To find the limit:
\[
\lim_{{x \to 1}} \frac{x^3 - 1}{x - 1}
\]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Factor the Numerator:**  
   Recognize that \( x^3 - 1 \) is a difference of cubes, which can be factored as:
   \[
   x^3 - 1 = (x - 1)(x^2 + x + 1)
   \]

2. **Simplify the Expression:**  
   Substitute the factored form into the original expression and cancel the common \( (x - 1) \) terms:
   \[
   \frac{x^3 - 1}{x - 1} = \frac{(x - 1)(x^2 + x + 1)}{x - 1} = x^2 + x + 1 \quad \text{for } x \neq 1
   \]

3. **Evaluate the Limit:**  
   Now, substitute \( x = 1 \) into the simplified expression:
   \[
   \lim_{{x \to 1}} (x^2 + x + 1) = 1^2 + 1 + 1 = 3
   \]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{3}
\]

Applying L'Hospital's Rule involves taking the derivatives of the numerator and the denominator separately.

The derivative of the numerator \( 1 - \cos x \) with respect to \( x \) is \( \sin x \).

The derivative of the denominator \( x^2 \) with respect to \( x \) is \( 2x \).

\[
\lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\sin x}{2x}
\]

The derivative of \( 2x \) with respect to \( x \) is \( 2 \).

\[
\lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\cos x}{2}
\]

Step-by-step explanation:

   - g(x) = x³ ⇒ g'(x) = 3x²

The derivative of \( \frac{1}{x} \) with respect to \( x \) can be found using basic differentiation rules. 

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Express the Function:**
   The function given is \( f(x) = \frac{1}{x} \).

2. **Rewrite Using Negative Exponents:**
   It's easier to differentiate if we rewrite it as \( f(x) = x^{-1} \).

3. **Apply the Power Rule:**
   The power rule states that if \( f(x) = x^n \), then \( f'(x) = n \cdot x^{n-1} \).
   
4. **Differentiate:**
   Here, \( n = -1 \). Applying the power rule:
   \[
   f'(x) = (-1) \cdot x^{-1 - 1} = -x^{-2}
   \]

5. **Simplify:**
   Convert back to fractional form if needed:
   \[
   f'(x) = -\frac{1}{x^2}
   \]

**Final Answer:** {Final Answer: "-1/x²"}

The derivative of f(x) = sin(x) is calculated as follows:

The problem is to find the derivative of \( e^x \) with respect to \( x \). 

**Solution:**

1. **Identify the function:** The given function is \( f(x) = e^x \), where \( e \) is Euler's number (approximately 2.71828).

2. **Apply the derivative rule for exponential functions:** 
   - The general rule states that the derivative of \( a^x \) with respect to \( x \) is \( a^x \ln(a) \), where \( a > 0 \).
   - For \( a = e \), since \( \ln(e) = 1 \), this simplifies the derivative.

3. **Compute the derivative:**
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} (e^x) = e^x \cdot \ln(e) = e^x \cdot 1 = e^x
   \]

**Final Answer:**  
The derivative of \( e^x \) with respect to \( x \) is \( e^x \).

F'(x) = x · e^x + e^x · 1

\[
F'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
\]

- Let \( u(x) = x \), so \( u'(x) = 1 \)
- Let \( v(x) = e^x \), so \( v'(x) = e^x \)

\[
F'(x) = 1 \cdot e^x + x \cdot e^x
\]

\[
F'(x) = (1 + x) \cdot e^x
\]

To find the derivative of the function \( F(x) = \frac{1}{x^2 + 1} \), we can use the chain rule. Here's a step-by-step explanation:

**Step 1: Rewrite the Function**

First, rewrite the function in a form that makes differentiation easier:
\[
F(x) = (x^2 + 1)^{-1}
\]

**Step 2: Apply the Chain Rule**

The chain rule states that if \( F(x) = g(h(x)) \), then the derivative \( F'(x) = g'(h(x)) \cdot h'(x) \).

- Let \( g(u) = u^{-1} \)
- Let \( h(x) = x^2 + 1 \)

**Step 3: Differentiate the Outer Function**

Differentiate \( g(u) = u^{-1} \) with respect to \( u \):
\[
g'(u) = -u^{-2}
\]

**Step 4: Differentiate the Inner Function**

Differentiate \( h(x) = x^2 + 1 \) with respect to \( x \):
\[
h'(x) = 2x
\]

**Step 5: Combine the Derivatives Using the Chain Rule**

Multiply the derivatives obtained from steps 3 and 4:
\[
F'(x) = g'(h(x)) \cdot h'(x) = - (x^2 + 1)^{-2} \cdot 2x
\]

**Step 6: Simplify the Expression**

Simplify the expression by writing it back in fractional form:
\[
F'(x) = -\frac{2x}{(x^2 + 1)^2}
\]

**Final Answer**
\[
\boxed{-\dfrac{2 x}{\left(x^{2}+1\right)^{2}}}
\]

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = \ln(\sin x) \), we'll use the chain rule. 

1. **Identify the functions**:  
   - Outer function: \( \ln(u) \)
   - Inner function: \( u = \sin x \)

2. **Differentiate each part**:  
   - Derivative of the outer function with respect to \( u \): \( \frac{d}{du} \ln(u) = \frac{1}{u} \)
   - Derivative of the inner function with respect to \( x \): \( \frac{d}{dx} \sin x = \cos x \)

   \]

To find the second derivative of \( F(x) = x^3 \ln x \), we first compute the first derivative using the product rule:

\[
F'(x) = \frac{d}{dx}(x^3) \cdot \ln x + x^3 \cdot \frac{d}{dx}(\ln x) = 3x^2 \ln x + x^3 \cdot \frac{1}{x} = 3x^2 \ln x + x^2

For the term \( 3x^2 \ln x \), apply the product rule again:

\[
\frac{d}{dx}(3x^2 \ln x) = 6x \ln x + 3x
\]

\[
F''(x) = 6x \ln x + 3x + 2x = 6x \ln x + 5x

The derivative of F(x) = \(\frac{e^x}{1 + e^x}\) can be found using either the product rule or the quotient rule.

Using the quotient rule:
- Let \( u = e^x \) and \( v = 1 + e^x \).
- Then, \( u' = e^x \) and \( v' = e^x \).
- Applying the quotient rule: 
  \[
  F'(x) = \frac{u'v - uv'}{v^2} = \frac{e^x(1 + e^x) - e^x \cdot e^x}{(1 + e^x)^2}
  \]
- Simplifying the numerator: \( e^x(1 + e^x) - e^{2x} = e^x + e^{2x} - e^{2x} = e^x \).
- Thus, 
  \[
  F'(x) = \frac{e^x}{(1 + e^x)^2}
  \]

So the final answer is:

The derivative of \(\tan^{-1} x\) with respect to \(x\) can be found using differentiation rules for inverse trigonometric functions.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Let \(y = \tan^{-1} x\):**
   - This implies that \( \tan y = x \).

2. **Differentiate both sides with respect to \(x\):**
   - Using the chain rule on the left side:
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx} (\tan y) = \sec^2 y \cdot \frac{dy}{dx}
     \]
   - The right side differentiates directly:
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(x) = 1
     \]

3. **Set the derivatives equal to each other:**
   \[
   \sec^2 y \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} = 1
   \]

4. **Solve for \( \frac{dy}{dx} \):**
   \[
   \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{\sec^2 y}
   \]

5. **Express \( \sec^2 y \) in terms of \(x\):**
   - Since \( \tan y = x \), consider a right triangle where the opposite side is \(x\) and the adjacent side is \(1\). The hypotenuse becomes \( \sqrt{1 + x^2} \).
   - Therefore:
     \[
     \cos y = \frac{1}{\sqrt{1 + x^2}} \quad \text{and thus} \quad \sec y = \sqrt{1 + x^2}
     \]
   - Squaring both sides gives \( \sec^2 y = 1 + x^2 \).

6. **Substitute back into the derivative:**
   \[
   \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{1 + x^2}
   \]

**Final Answer:**

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = \frac{x}{1 + x^2} \), we'll use the **quotient rule**. 

The quotient rule states that if you have a function \( F(x) = \frac{u(x)}{v(x)} \), then its derivative is:

\[
F'(x) = \frac{u'(x)v(x) - u(x)v'(x)}{[v(x)]^2}
\]

**Step 1:** Identify \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \).

\[
u(x) = x \\
v(x) = 1 + x^2
\]

**Step 2:** Compute the derivatives of \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \).

\[
u'(x) = 1 \\
v'(x) = 2x
\]

**Step 3:** Apply the quotient rule.

\[
F'(x) = \frac{u'(x)v(x) - u(x)v'(x)}{[v(x)]^2} = \frac{1 \cdot (1 + x^2) - x \cdot 2x}{(1 + x^2)^2}
\]

**Step 4:** Simplify the numerator.

\[
1 + x^2 - 2x^2 = 1 - x^2
\]

So, the derivative is:

\[
F'(x) = \frac{1 - x^2}{(1 + x^2)^2}
\]

I need to find the limit as \( x \) approaches infinity of the rational function \( \frac{3x^2 + 2x + 1}{5x^2 + 4x + 2} \).

To find the limit:

\[
\lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{3x^2 + 2x + 1}{5x^2 + 4x + 2}
\]

**Step 1: Identify the degrees of the numerator and denominator**

Both the numerator \( 3x^2 + 2x + 1 \) and the denominator \( 5x^2 + 4x + 2 \) are polynomials of degree 2.

**Step 2: Apply the limit rule for rational functions**

When the degrees of the numerator and denominator are equal, the limit as \( x \) approaches infinity is the ratio of their leading coefficients.

- Leading coefficient of the numerator = 3
- Leading coefficient of the denominator = 5

**Step 3: Compute the limit**

\[

The limit as x approaches infinity of (1 + 1/x)^x is known to be Euler's number, e.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Recognize the Standard Limit:** The expression (1 + 1/x)^x is a well-known limit in calculus that evaluates to e.
   
2. **Verify with Logarithms:** To confirm, take the natural logarithm of the function:
   - Let y = (1 + 1/x)^x
   - ln(y) = x * ln(1 + 1/x)

3. **Approximate for Small t:**
   - Let t = 1/x, so as x → ∞, t → 0.
   - Use the Taylor series expansion of ln(1 + t): ln(1+t) ≈ t - t²/2 + t³/3 - ...
   - Substitute back into ln(y): ln(y) ≈ x*(t - t²/2 + ...) = x*(1/x - 1/(2x²) + ...)
   
4. **Simplify the Expression:**
   - ln(y) ≈ 1 - 1/(2x) + higher-order terms.
   - As x → ∞, all terms with 1/x go to zero, so lim_{x→∞} ln(y) = 1.

5. **Exponentiate to Find the Limit:**
   - Since lim_{x→∞} ln(y) = 1, then lim_{x→∞} y = e^1 = e.

6. **Confirm with L’Hôpital’s Rule (Alternative Approach):**
   - Rewrite ln(y) as [ln(1 + 1/x)] / (1/x), which is a 0/0 form.
   - Apply derivatives:
     - d/dx [ln(1 + 1/x)] = (-1)/(x²(1 + 1/x))
     - d/dx [1/x] = -1/x²
   - The limit becomes [(-1)/(x²(1 + 1/x))] / (-1/x²) = 1/(1 + 0) = 1.
   - Therefore, lim_{x→∞} ln(y) = 1 → y approaches e.

**Final Answer:**

The limit evaluates to Euler's number e.

Numerator: f(x) = ln x

The limit of \(\frac{\ln x}{x}\) as \(x\) approaches infinity is calculated by recognizing that both the numerator and denominator approach infinity. Applying L'Hospital's Rule once gives us the derivative of the numerator over the derivative of the denominator, resulting in \(\frac{1/x}{1} = \frac{1}{x}\), which approaches 0 as \(x\) tends to infinity.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Form:** As \(x \to \infty\), both \(\ln x\) and \(x\) approach infinity, resulting in an indeterminate form of \(\frac{\infty}{\infty}\).

2. **Apply L'Hospital's Rule:** Differentiate numerator and denominator.
   - Derivative of \(\ln x\) is \(\frac{1}{x}\).
   - Derivative of \(x\) is 1.

3. **Evaluate the New Limit:**
   \[
   \lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{\frac{1}{x}}{1} = \lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{1}{x} = 0
   \]

**Final Answer:** 0

The limit evaluates to 1 by applying L'Hospital's Rule or by expanding around x=1.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

We are to find:
\[ \lim_{x \to 1} \frac{x\sqrt{x} - \sqrt{x}}{x - 1} \]

Simplify the expression:

First, rewrite \( x\sqrt{x} = x^{3/2} \) and \( \sqrt{x} = x^{1/2} \).

So,
\[ \lim_{x \to 1} \frac{x^{3/2} - x^{1/2}}{x - 1} \]

At \( x=1 \), both numerator and denominator are zero, so we can apply L'Hospital's Rule.

Differentiate the numerator:
\[ \frac{d}{dx}(x^{3/2}) = \frac{3}{2}x^{1/2}, \quad \frac{d}{dx}(x^{1/2}) = \frac{1}{2}x^{-1/2} \]
So, derivative of numerator:
\[ \frac{3}{2}\sqrt{x} - \frac{1}{2\sqrt{x}} \]

Derivative of denominator is 1.

Apply L'Hospital's Rule:
\[ \lim_{x \to 1} \left( \frac{3}{2}\sqrt{x} - \frac{1}{2\sqrt{x}} \right) \]
At \( x=1 \):
\[ \frac{3}{2}(1) - \frac{1}{2}(1) = \frac{3}{2} - \frac{1}{2} = 1 \]

Thus, the limit is **1**.

Answer:  
\boxed{1}

To find the derivative of the function \( \frac{d}{dx}(x^3 + 2x^2 + x + 1) \), we will differentiate each term individually using basic differentiation rules.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Differentiate \( x^3 \):**
   - Using the power rule, the derivative of \( x^n \) is \( nx^{n-1} \).
   - So, \( \frac{d}{dx}(x^3) = 3x^2 \).

2. **Differentiate \( 2x^2 \):**
   - Apply the constant multiple rule: \( \frac{d}{dx}[c \cdot f(x)] = c \cdot f'(x) \).
   - Differentiate \( x^2 \) to get \( 2x \), then multiply by the constant 2.
   - So, \( \frac{d}{dx}(2x^2) = 4x \).

3. **Differentiate \( x \):**
   - The derivative of \( x \) with respect to \( x \) is 1.
   - So, \( \frac{d}{dx}(x) = 1 \).

4. **Differentiate the constant term \( 1 \):**
   - The derivative of any constant is 0.
   - So, \( \frac{d}{dx}(1) = 0 \).

5. **Combine all the derivatives:**
   - Adding up all the individual derivatives:
   - \( 3x^2 + 4x + 1 + 0 = 3x^2 + 4x + 1 \).

**Final Answer:**

The derivative of \( x^3 + 2x^2 + x + 1 \) with respect to \( x \) is:

\[
\boxed{3x^2 + 4x + 1}
\]

***********************<think>
To find the derivative of F(x) = tan(x), I recall that the derivative of tan(x) is a standard result from calculus.

I know that tan(x) can be expressed as sin(x)/cos(x). Using this, I can apply the quotient rule to differentiate it.

Applying the quotient rule: 

d/dx [tan(x)] = d/dx [sin(x)/cos(x)] 
= [cos(x)*cos(x) - (-sin(x))*sin(x)] / cos²(x)
= [cos²(x) + sin²(x)] / cos²(x)

Since cos²(x) + sin²(x) equals 1, this simplifies to:

d/dx [tan(x)] = 1 / cos²(x) 
= sec²(x)

Therefore, the derivative of tan(x) is sec²(x).
</think>

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = \tan(x) \), we can use standard differentiation rules.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Expressing Tangent in Terms of Sine and Cosine:**
   - Recall that \( \tan(x) = \frac{\sin(x)}{\cos(x)} \).

2. **Applying the Quotient Rule:**
   - The quotient rule states that if you have a function \( f(x) = \frac{g(x)}{h(x)} \), its derivative is:
     \[
     f'(x) = \frac{g'(x)h(x) - g(x)h'(x)}{[h(x)]^2}
     \]
   - Here, \( g(x) = \sin(x) \) and \( h(x) = \cos(x) \).

3. **Differentiating Numerator and Denominator:**
   - \( g'(x) = \cos(x) \)
   - \( h'(x) = -\sin(x) \)

4. **Plugging into the Quotient Rule Formula:**
   \[
   f'(x) = \frac{\cos(x)\cdot\cos(x) - \sin(x)\cdot(-\sin(x))}{\cos^2(x)} 
   \]
   
5. **Simplifying the Expression:**
   \[
   f'(x) = \frac{\cos^2(x) + \sin^2(x)}{\cos^2(x)}
   \]

6. **Using Pythagorean Identity:**
   - \( \cos^2(x) + \sin^2(x) = 1 \)
   
7. **Final Simplification:**
   \[
   f'(x) = \frac{1}{\cos^2(x)} = \sec^2(x)
   \]

**Answer:**  
The derivative of \( F(x) = \tan(x) \) is \( F'(x) = \sec^2(x) \).

```json
{Final Answer: "sec²(x)"}
```

The product rule states that the derivative of u*v is u’v + uv’. Here, u = x and v = ln(x). So, du/dx is 1, because the derivative of x with respect to x is 1. And dv/dx is 1/x, since the derivative of ln(x) is 1 over x.

Putting that together, the derivative of the right side becomes (1)*ln(x) + x*(1/x). Simplifying that gives ln(x) + 1 because x*(1/x) is just 1. So now I have:

(1/y) * dy/dx = ln(x) + 1

To solve for dy/dx, I multiply both sides by y. Since y was originally defined as x^x, substituting that back in gives:

dy/dx = y*(ln(x) + 1) = x^x*(ln(x) + 1)

Wait, let me make sure I didn't make any mistakes here. Let's go through the steps again quickly. Start with y = x^x, take ln both sides: ln(y) = x*ln(x). Differentiate both sides:

Left side: (1/y)y' 

Right side: derivative of x is 1 times ln(x) plus x times derivative of ln(x), which is 1/x. So that's correct.

So putting it all together, the derivative dy/dx equals x^x multiplied by (ln(x) + 1). That seems right.
</think>

The derivative of \( x^x \) with respect to \( x \) can be found using logarithmic differentiation.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Let \( y = x^x \)**:
   
2. **Take the natural logarithm of both sides**:
   \[
   \ln(y) = \ln(x^x)
   \]
   
3. **Simplify using logarithmic properties**:
   \[
   \ln(y) = x \cdot \ln(x)
   \]
   
4. **Differentiate both sides with respect to \( x \)**:
   - Left side: Use the chain rule.
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}[\ln(y)] = \frac{1}{y} \cdot \frac{dy}{dx}
     \]
   - Right side: Apply the product rule on \( x \cdot \ln(x) \).
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}[x \cdot \ln(x)] = 1 \cdot \ln(x) + x \cdot \frac{1}{x} = \ln(x) + 1
     \]
     
5. **Set the derivatives equal**:
   \[
   \frac{1}{y} \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} = \ln(x) + 1
   \]
   
6. **Solve for \( \frac{dy}{dx} \)** by multiplying both sides by \( y \):
   \[
   \frac{dy}{dx} = y \cdot (\ln(x) + 1)
   \]
   
7. **Substitute back \( y = x^x \)**:
   \[
   \frac{dy}{dx} = x^x \cdot (\ln(x) + 1)
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{x^x(\ln(x) + 1)}

To find the second derivative of \( F(x) = x^2 e^x \), we'll follow these steps:

1. **First Derivative (\( F'(x) \))**:
   
   We apply the product rule for differentiation, which states that if \( u(x) = f(x)g(x) \), then \( u'(x) = f'(x)g(x) + f(x)g'(x) \).
   
   - Let \( f(x) = x^2 \) and \( g(x) = e^x \).
   - Then, \( f'(x) = 2x \) and \( g'(x) = e^x \).

   Applying the product rule:
   \[
   F'(x) = f'(x)g(x) + f(x)g'(x) = 2x e^x + x^2 e^x
   \]

2. **Second Derivative (\( F''(x) \))**:
   
   Now, we differentiate \( F'(x) = 2x e^x + x^2 e^x \).

   - Differentiate the first term \( 2x e^x \):
     - Let \( f(x) = 2x \) and \( g(x) = e^x \).
     - \( f'(x) = 2 \) and \( g'(x) = e^x \).
     - Applying the product rule: \( 2 e^x + 2x e^x \).

   - Differentiate the second term \( x^2 e^x \):
     - Let \( f(x) = x^2 \) and \( g(x) = e^x \).
     - \( f'(x) = 2x \) and \( g'(x) = e^x \).
     - Applying the product rule: \( 2x e^x + x^2 e^x \).

   Combining these results:
   \[
   F''(x) = (2 e^x + 2x e^x) + (2x e^x + x^2 e^x)
   \]
   
   Simplify by combining like terms:
   \[
   F''(x) = 2 e^x + 4x e^x + x^2 e^x
   \]

   Factor out \( e^x \):
   \[
   F''(x) = (x^2 + 4x + 2) e^x
   \]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{(x^{2}+4x+2)e^{x}}
\]

The derivative of \( F(x) = \frac{1}{x^2 + 1} \) can be found using either the quotient rule or recognizing it as a composite function and applying the chain rule.

**Using the Quotient Rule:**
- Let \( g(x) = 1 \) and \( h(x) = x^2 + 1 \).
- \( g'(x) = 0 \) (since the derivative of a constant is zero).
- \( h'(x) = 2x \).
- Applying the quotient rule: 
  \[
  F'(x) = \frac{g'(x)h(x) - g(x)h'(x)}{[h(x)]^2} = \frac{0 \cdot (x^2 + 1) - 1 \cdot 2x}{(x^2 + 1)^2} = \frac{-2x}{(x^2 + 1)^2}.
  \]

**Using the Chain Rule:**
- Let \( u = x^2 + 1 \), so \( F(x) = \frac{1}{u} = u^{-1} \).
- The derivative of \( u^{-1} \) with respect to \( u \) is \( -u^{-2} \).
- Multiply by the derivative of \( u \) with respect to \( x \): 
  \[
  \frac{dF}{dx} = -u^{-2} \cdot \frac{du}{dx} = -\frac{1}{(x^2 + 1)^2} \cdot 2x = \frac{-2x}{(x^2 + 1)^2}.
  \]

Both methods yield the same result.

**Final Answer:**
The derivative of \( F(x) = \frac{1}{x^2 + 1} \) is:
\[
F'(x) = \boxed{\dfrac{-2x}{(x^{2}+1)^{2}}}
\]

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = \ln(\sin x) \), we'll use the chain rule.

**Step 1:** Identify the outer and inner functions.
- **Outer function:** Natural logarithm, \( f(u) = \ln(u) \)
- **Inner function:** Trigonometric sine function, \( u = g(x) = \sin x \)

**Step 2:** Differentiate each function separately.
- The derivative of the outer function with respect to \( u \) is:
  \[
  f'(u) = \frac{1}{u}
  \]
- The derivative of the inner function with respect to \( x \) is:
  \[
  g'(x) = \cos x
  \]

**Step 3:** Apply the chain rule by multiplying the derivatives.
\[
F'(x) = f'(g(x)) \cdot g'(x) = \frac{1}{\sin x} \cdot \cos x = \frac{\cos x}{\sin x}
\]
Simplify using trigonometric identities:
\[
\frac{\cos x}{\sin x} = \cot x
\]

**Final Answer:**
\[
F'(x) = \cot x
\]

The derivative of the function \( F(x) = \frac{e^x}{1 + e^x} \) can be found using the quotient rule.

- \( u(x) = e^x \)
- \( v(x) = 1 + e^x \)

- \( v'(x) = e^x \)

Applying the quotient rule:
\[
F'(x) = \frac{u'v - uv'}{v^2} = \frac{e^x(1 + e^x) - e^x(e^x)}{(1 + e^x)^2}
\]

Simplifying the numerator:
\[
e^x(1 + e^x) - e^x e^x = e^x + e^{2x} - e^{2x} = e^x
\]

Thus, the derivative is:
\[
F'(x) = \frac{e^x}{(1 + e^x)^2}
\]

**Answer:** {Final Answer: "e^x/(1 + e^x)^2"}

The derivative of arctan(x) with respect to x can be found using the chain rule and trigonometric identities.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Let y = arctan(x):**
   - This implies that tan(y) = x.

2. **Differentiate both sides with respect to x:**
   - d/dx [tan(y)] = d/dx [x].

3. **Apply the chain rule on the left side:**
   - sec²(y) * dy/dx = 1.

4. **Solve for dy/dx:**
   - dy/dx = 1 / sec²(y).

5. **Express sec²(y) in terms of x using a right triangle:**
   - If tan(y) = x, then construct a right triangle where the opposite side is x and the adjacent side is 1.
   - The hypotenuse would be √(1 + x²).
   - Therefore, sec(y) = hypotenuse / adjacent = √(1 + x²)/1.

6. **Square sec(y) to find sec²(y):**
   - sec²(y) = (√(1 + x²))² = 1 + x².

7. **Substitute back into dy/dx:**
   - dy/dx = 1 / (1 + x²).

**Final Answer:**

The derivative of arctan(x) with respect to x is \(\frac{1}{1 + x^2}\).

The derivative of \( F(x) = \frac{x}{1 + x^2} \) can be found using the quotient rule. Here's how:

1. **Identify f(x) and g(x):**
   - \( f(x) = x \)
   - \( g(x) = 1 + x^2 \)

2. **Find their derivatives:**
   - \( f'(x) = 1 \)
   - \( g'(x) = 2x \)

3. **Apply the quotient rule formula:**
   \[
   F'(x) = \frac{f'(x)g(x) - f(x)g'(x)}{[g(x)]^2}
   \]

   \[

   \]

   \[
   1 - x^2
   \]

6. **Final derivative:**
   \[
   F'(x) = \frac{1 - x^2}{(1 + x^2)^2}
   \]

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

   We need to evaluate the limit of the function \(2x^2 - 5x + 1\) as \(x\) approaches 3.

2. **Identify the Type of Function:**
   The given function is a polynomial (specifically, a quadratic function).

3. **Evaluate Continuity:**
   Polynomial functions are continuous for all real numbers. Therefore, the limit can be found by directly substituting the value that \(x\) approaches into the function.

4. **Substitute and Simplify:**
   \[
   \lim_{{x \to 3}} (2x^2 - 5x + 1) = 2(3)^2 - 5(3) + 1
   \]
   \[
   = 2 \times 9 - 15 + 1
   \]
   \[
   = 18 - 15 + 1
   \]
   \[
   = 4
   \]

5. **Conclusion:**
   The limit of the function as \(x\) approaches 3 is 4.

The limit as x approaches 0 of sin(x)/x can be found using the squeeze theorem or recognizing it as a standard limit result.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Understand the Problem:** We need to evaluate limₓ→₀ (sin x)/x.

2. **Direct Substitution:** Plugging in x = 0 gives 0/0, which is indeterminate. So we need another method.

3. **Use Squeeze Theorem:**
   - For small positive angles, sin x ≤ x.
   - Dividing by x (positive), we get sin x /x ≤ 1.
   - Also, using inequalities involving cos x and sin x around the unit circle:
     \[ \cos x \leq \frac{\sin x}{x} \leq 1 \]
   
4. **Evaluate Limits:**
   - As x → 0⁺, cos x → 1, so both bounds approach 1.
   - By squeeze theorem, limₓ→₀⁺ (sin x)/x = 1.

5. **Consider Negative Approach:**
   - For x approaching 0 from the left, sin(-x) / (-x) = sin x /x remains the same as positive case due to symmetry and odd/even properties.
   - Hence, limₓ→₀⁻ (sin x)/x = 1.

6. **Conclusion:** Since both one-sided limits equal 1, the overall limit is 1.

**Final Answer:**

The limit of sin(x)/x as x approaches 0 is 1.

The given problem asks for the limit of \(\frac{x^3 - 1}{x - 1}\) as \(x\) approaches 1.

To solve this, we first recognize that substituting \(x = 1\) results in a \(\frac{0}{0}\) indeterminate form. To resolve this, we factor the numerator:

\[
x^3 - 1 = (x - 1)(x^2 + x + 1)
\]

This allows us to simplify the expression:

\[
\frac{(x - 1)(x^2 + x + 1)}{x - 1} = x^2 + x + 1 \quad \text{for } x \neq 1
\]

Now, taking the limit as \(x\) approaches 1 gives:

\[
\lim_{x \to 1} (x^2 + x + 1) = 1^2 + 1 + 1 = 3
\]

**Answer:** {Final Answer: "3"}

   \( \lim_{{x \to 2}} \frac{x^2 - 4}{x - 2} \)

So apply L'Hospital's Rule a third time: derivative of sin(x) is cos(x), and derivative of 6x is 6. So now the limit becomes [cos(x)] / 6. As x approaches 0, cos(0) is 1, so this limit is 1/6.

Both methods give me the same result. The Taylor series expansion approach gave me (x^3)/6 divided by x^3 equals 1/6, and L'Hospital's Rule three times also led to 1/6. So I'm confident that the limit is 1/6.
</think>

The limit as \( x \) approaches 0 of \( \frac{x - \sin x}{x^3} \) can be found using either Taylor series expansion or L'Hospital's Rule, both leading to the result \( \frac{1}{6} \).

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Form:** As \( x \to 0 \), both numerator (\( x - \sin x \)) and denominator (\( x^3 \)) approach 0, resulting in a \( \frac{0}{0} \) indeterminate form.

2. **Apply Taylor Series Expansion:**
   - The Taylor series for \( \sin x \) around 0 is:
     \[
     \sin x = x - \frac{x^3}{6} + \cdots
     \]
   - Subtracting from \( x \):
     \[
     x - \sin x = x - \left( x - \frac{x^3}{6} \right) = \frac{x^3}{6}
     \]
   - Substitute into the limit:
     \[
     \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\frac{x^3}{6}}{x^3} = \frac{1}{6}
     \]

3. **Alternatively, Use L'Hospital's Rule:**
   - First application (since it's \( \frac{0}{0} \)):
     \[
     \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{1 - \cos x}{3x^2}
     \]
   - Second application (still \( \frac{0}{0} \)):
     \[
     \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\sin x}{6x}
     \]
   - Third application:
     \[
     \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\cos x}{6} = \frac{1}{6}
     \]

**Answer:**
The limit is \( \frac{1}{6} \).

The limit as \( x \) approaches 0 from the positive side of \( x^x \) is calculated by taking the natural logarithm, applying L'Hospital's Rule, and simplifying. The result is found to be 1.

Step-by-step explanation:

1. Let \( y = x^x \).
2. Take the natural logarithm: \( \ln(y) = x \ln(x) \).
3. Find the limit of \( \ln(y) \) as \( x \) approaches 0+: 
   - Rewrite \( x \ln(x) \) as \( \frac{\ln(x)}{1/x} \), which is \( (-\infty)/\infty \), an indeterminate form.
4. Apply L'Hospital's Rule:
   - Differentiate numerator: \( d/dx [\ln(x)] = 1/x \).
   - Differentiate denominator: \( d/dx [1/x] = -1/x^2 \).
5. The limit becomes \( (1/x) / (-1/x^2) = -x \), which approaches 0 as \( x \) approaches 0+.
6. Therefore, \( \ln(y) \) approaches 0, so \( y \) approaches \( e^0 = 1 \).

Answer: The limit is 1.

   \[
   \frac{x^2 + x + 1}{x^2 - x + 1} = \frac{1 + \frac{1}{x} + \frac{1}{x^2}}{1 - \frac{1}{x} + \frac{1}{x^2}}

To find the derivative of F(x) = 3x² - 5x + 4, I'll apply basic differentiation rules.

First, differentiate each term separately. The derivative of 3x² is found using the power rule: bring down the exponent as a coefficient and reduce it by one, resulting in 6x.

Next, the derivative of -5x is simply -5, since the derivative of x with respect to x is 1.

Combining these results, the derivative F'(x) becomes 6x - 5.

To find the derivative of the function \( F(x) = 3x^2 - 5x + 4 \), we will use basic differentiation rules. Here's a step-by-step solution:

1. **Differentiate each term separately:**

   - For the term \( 3x^2 \):
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(3x^2) = 3 \times 2x^{2-1} = 6x
     \]
     
   - For the term \( -5x \):
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(-5x) = -5 \times 1 = -5
     \]
     
   - For the constant term \( 4 \):
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(4) = 0
     \]

2. **Combine the derivatives of each term:**
   \[
   F'(x) = 6x - 5 + 0 = 6x - 5
   \]

**Final Answer:**  
\(\boxed{F'(x) = 6x - 5}\)

The derivative of \( \frac{1}{x} \) with respect to \( x \) is calculated using the limit definition. 

Step-by-step explanation:
1. Start with the function \( f(x) = \frac{1}{x} \).
2. Apply the limit definition of the derivative: 
   \[
   f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{\frac{1}{x+h} - \frac{1}{x}}{h}
   \]
3. Simplify the numerator by finding a common denominator:
   \[
   \frac{x - (x + h)}{x(x + h)} = \frac{-h}{x(x + h)}
   \]
4. Substitute back into the limit expression and simplify:
   \[
   \frac{-h}{x(x + h) \cdot h} = \frac{-1}{x(x + h)}
   \]
5. Take the limit as \( h \) approaches 0, resulting in:
   \[
   f'(x) = -\frac{1}{x^2}
   \]

The problem requires finding the derivative of \( e^x \) with respect to \( x \). 

Step-by-step explanation:

1. **Identify the function**: The given function is \( f(x) = e^x \), where \( e \) is Euler's number approximately equal to 2.71828.

2. **Apply the derivative rule for exponential functions**: The derivative of \( e^x \) with respect to \( x \) is a well-known result in calculus. Specifically, if \( f(x) = e^{u(x)} \), then \( f'(x) = e^{u(x)} \cdot u'(x) \). In this case, \( u(x) = x \), so \( u'(x) = 1 \).

3. **Compute the derivative**: Applying the rule, we get:
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} (e^x) = e^x \cdot 1 = e^x
   \]

Therefore, the derivative of \( e^x \) with respect to \( x \) is \( e^x \).

**Final Answer:**

The derivative of \( x^x \) with respect to \( x \) is found using logarithmic differentiation.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Let \( y = x^x \).**
2. Take natural logarithm on both sides:
   \[
   \ln(y) = \ln(x^x)
   \]
3. Apply logarithmic identity to simplify the right side:
   \[
   \ln(y) = x \cdot \ln(x)
   \]
4. Differentiate both sides with respect to \( x \):
   - Left side: Use chain rule.
     \[
     \frac{1}{y} \cdot \frac{dy}{dx}
     \]
   - Right side: Apply product rule on \( x \cdot \ln(x) \).
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}[x] \cdot \ln(x) + x \cdot \frac{d}{dx}[\ln(x)] = \ln(x) + 1
     \]
5. Equate the derivatives:
   \[
   \frac{1}{y} \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} = \ln(x) + 1
   \]
6. Solve for \( \frac{dy}{dx} \):
   \[
   \frac{dy}{dx} = y \cdot (\ln(x) + 1)
   \]
7. Substitute back \( y = x^x \):
   \[
   \frac{dy}{dx} = x^x \cdot (\ln(x) + 1)
   \]

**Final Answer:**
The derivative of \( x^x \) with respect to \( x \) is \( x^x (1 + \ln(x)) \).

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = \frac{e^x}{1 + e^x} \), we'll use the **quotient rule**. The quotient rule states that if you have a function \( \frac{u(x)}{v(x)} \), its derivative is:

\[
F'(x) = \frac{u'(x)v(x) - u(x)v'(x)}{[v(x)]^2}
\]

Let's apply this to our function:

1. **Identify \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \):**
   \[
   u(x) = e^x \\
   v(x) = 1 + e^x
   \]

2. **Find the derivatives of \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \):**
   \[
   u'(x) = e^x \\
   v'(x) = e^x
   \]

3. **Apply the quotient rule:**
   \[
   F'(x) = \frac{e^x(1 + e^x) - e^x(e^x)}{(1 + e^x)^2}
   \]

4. **Simplify the numerator:**
   \[
   e^x(1 + e^x) - e^x(e^x) = e^x + (e^x)^2 - (e^x)^2 = e^x
   \]

5. **Final simplified form of the derivative:**
   \[
   F'(x) = \frac{e^x}{(1 + e^x)^2}
   \]

**Answer:**  
\boxed{\dfrac{e^{x}}{(1+e^{x})^{2}}}

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = x \ln x \), we use the product rule. Let:

\[
u(x) = x \quad \text{and} \quad v(x) = \ln x
\]

The derivatives are:
\[
u'(x) = 1 \quad \text{and} \quad v'(x) = \frac{1}{x}
\]

Applying the product rule:
\[
F'(x) = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x) = (1)(\ln x) + x\left(\frac{1}{x}\right)
\]
Simplifying:
\[
F'(x) = \ln x + 1
\]

**Final Answer:**  
The derivative of \( F(x) = x \ln x \) is \( \boxed{\ln x + 1} \).

The second derivative of F(x) = e^{x^2} is calculated by first finding the first derivative using the chain rule and then applying the product rule to differentiate the resulting expression.

Step-by-Step Explanation:
1. First Derivative (F’(x)):
   - Let u = x², so d/dx(e^{u}) = e^{u} * du/dx.
   - du/dx = 2x.
   - Therefore, F’(x) = 2x * e^{x^2}.

2. Second Derivative (F''(x)):
   - Apply the product rule to F’(x): d/dx(fg) = f’g + fg’, where f(x) = 2x and g(x) = e^{x²}.
   - Compute f’(x) = 2.
   - Compute g’(x) using chain rule: dg/dx = 2x * e^{x²}.
   - Therefore, F''(x) = (2)(e^{x²}) + (2x)(2x * e^{x²}).
   - Simplify: 2e^{x²} + 4x²e^{x²} = e^{x²}(2 + 4x²).

Answer: {Final Answer: "2(1 + 2x²)e^{x²}"}

The derivative of the function \( F(x) = \frac{x}{1 + x^2} \) can be found using the quotient rule.

**Solution:**
Let:
- \( u(x) = x \)
- \( v(x) = 1 + x^2 \)

Then:
- \( u'(x) = 1 \)
- \( v'(x) = 2x \)

Applying the quotient rule:
\[
F'(x) = \frac{u'(x)v(x) - u(x)v'(x)}{[v(x)]^2} = \frac{(1)(1 + x^2) - (x)(2x)}{(1 + x^2)^2}
\]
Simplifying the numerator:
\[
(1)(1 + x^2) - 2x^2 = 1 + x^2 - 2x^2 = 1 - x^2
\]
Thus, the derivative is:
\[
F'(x) = \frac{1 - x^2}{(1 + x^2)^2}
\]

**Final Answer:**
The derivative of \( F(x) = \frac{x}{1 + x^2} \) is \( \boxed{\dfrac{1 - x^{2}}{(1 + x^{2})^{2}}} \).

The derivative of \(\sec^2 x\) with respect to \(x\) can be found using differentiation rules. By applying the chain rule directly or by using trigonometric identities, we determine that the derivative is \(2 \tan x \sec^2 x\).

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Function:** We are given \(f(x) = \sec^2 x\).

2. **Apply the Chain Rule:** Let’s consider \(u = \sec x\). Then, \(f(x) = u^2\).
   - The derivative of \(u^2\) with respect to \(u\) is \(2u\).
   - Now, find the derivative of \(u = \sec x\) with respect to \(x\), which is \(\sec x \tan x\).

3. **Combine Using Chain Rule:**
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} (\sec^2 x) = 2 \sec x \cdot (\sec x \tan x) = 2 \sec^2 x \tan x
   \]

4. **Alternative Approach (Using Identity):** 
   - Recall that \(\sec^2 x = \tan^2 x + 1\).
   - Differentiate term by term:
     - The derivative of \(\tan^2 x\) is \(2 \tan x \cdot \sec^2 x\) using the chain rule.
     - The derivative of 1 is 0.

5. **Final Result:** Both methods lead to the same result, confirming that:
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} (\sec^2 x) = 2 \tan x \sec^2 x
   \]

**Final Answer:**
The derivative of \(\sec^2 x\) with respect to \(x\) is \(2 \tan x \sec^2 x\).
